If I’m reviewing an audit file, something like a Slack message, or someone talking outside my office, can completely pull me away from it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Even my own thoughts—like suddenly thinking about marathon training—can derail me, and then I have to work to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get distracted in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside work too. I can be doing something at home and a small thing pulls me away—like a notification, a noise, or even just a random thought about my training—and then I’m completely off track. It’s not just at the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I have to sit still for a long review, it starts to feel physically uncomfortable, like pressure building up inside me. I’ll end up standing up, walking to the printer, or finding some reason to move, even if I don’t really need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as acting impulsively a lot, at least not in a way that stands out to me. I’d probably need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I catch myself jumping in before someone’s finished, especially with junior auditors. If I think I know the answer or I have an idea, it feels urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I really stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in other situations?\n\nPatient: Yes. Waiting is hard for me. Like if I’m in line at the courthouse records office or sitting in a clinic lobby, I get really tense and impatient. I keep checking my phone, or I’ll go over what I need to say in my head instead of just waiting calmly.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though what I remember more is what teachers wrote about me. Even in elementary school, my report cards would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through small details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I can get frustrated or overwhelmed, but I don’t usually feel like my mood is suddenly shifting in a big way for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much. It goes back to elementary school, from what I remember and from what my report cards said. Teachers would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still teases me that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting right in my backpack.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Sitting still for a long review feels physically uncomfortable, like pressure building inside me until I need to stand up, walk to the printer, or invent a reason to move.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even during things that are supposed to be relaxing, like reading at home or sitting quietly before a salsa class starts, I end up humming, checking something, or talking because pure quiet makes me antsy.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On weekends I tell my husband I'm going to rest, but then I bounce from laundry to email to a run to volunteer planning and don't really stop until I'm exhausted.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the courthouse records office or sitting in a clinic lobby makes me tense and impatient, and I keep checking my phone or rehearsing what I need to say instead of just waiting.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I catch myself jumping into conversations before people finish, especially with junior auditors, because the answer or idea feels urgent in my head and I blurt it out before I can stop myself.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my report cards said I was bright but lost assignments, rushed past small details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish work. My mother still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, my mistakes and unfinished follow-up have reached the point where a client review was affected and I worry people are questioning whether I can handle the promotion. At home, my husband has had to take over bills and commitments I agreed to because he cannot count on me to complete them reliably.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably interfering for about 1 year and 10 months, since the promotion after the firm-wide award made my workload and responsibilities much harder to juggle.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I can get frustrated or overwhelmed, but I don’t usually feel like my mood is suddenly shifting in a big way for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much. It goes back to elementary school, from what I remember and from what my report cards said. Teachers would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still teases me that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting right in my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember that being a problem, and honestly it still is. I can know exactly what I’m supposed to do and still miss a small detail—like a changed date, transposing a number, or leaving some placeholder note in a workpaper. It’s embarrassing, especially in accounting, because details are supposed to be my strength.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused you significant problems or stress in your personal or work life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having trouble following through on things at work. I’ll start follow-up items after audits or mentoring events with every intention of finishing them, and then a few days later I realize the final email, review note, or form never actually got done. It’s been frustrating, and it’s starting to feel like I need help figuring out why that keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily getting distracted while working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very easily. If I’m reviewing an audit file, something like a Slack message, or someone talking outside my office, can completely pull me away from it. Even my own thoughts—like suddenly thinking about marathon training—can derail me, and then I have to work to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get distracted in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside work too. I can be doing something at home and a small thing pulls me away—like a notification, a noise, or even just a random thought about my training—and then I’m completely off track. It’s not just at the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I have to sit still for a long review, it starts to feel physically uncomfortable, like pressure building up inside me. I’ll end up standing up, walking to the printer, or finding some reason to move, even if I don’t really need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as acting impulsively a lot, at least not in a way that stands out to me. I’d probably need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I catch myself jumping in before someone’s finished, especially with junior auditors. If I think I know the answer or I have an idea, it feels urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I really stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in other situations?\n\nPatient: Yes. Waiting is hard for me. Like if I’m in line at the courthouse records office or sitting in a clinic lobby, I get really tense and impatient. I keep checking my phone, or I’ll go over what I need to say in my head instead of just waiting calmly.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though what I remember more is what teachers wrote about me. Even in elementary school, my report cards would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through small details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I can get frustrated or overwhelmed, but I don’t usually feel like my mood is suddenly shifting in a big way for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much. It goes back to elementary school, from what I remember and from what my report cards said. Teachers would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still teases me that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting right in my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember that being a problem, and honestly it still is. I can know exactly what I’m supposed to do and still miss a small detail—like a changed date, transposing a number, or leaving some placeholder note in a workpaper. It’s embarrassing, especially in accounting, because details are supposed to be my strength.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused you significant problems or stress in your personal or work life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s caused real problems. At work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow up on have gotten serious enough that a client review was affected. And now I’m worried people are questioning whether I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAt home, it causes stress too. My husband has ended up taking over bills and commitments I agreed to handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. It’s frustrating and embarrassing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s been frustrating, and it’s starting to feel like I need help figuring out why that keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily getting distracted while working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very easily. If I’m reviewing an audit file, something like a Slack message, or someone talking outside my office, can completely pull me away from it. Even my own thoughts—like suddenly thinking about marathon training—can derail me, and then I have to work to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get distracted in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside work too. I can be doing something at home and a small thing pulls me away—like a notification, a noise, or even just a random thought about my training—and then I’m completely off track. It’s not just at the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I have to sit still for a long review, it starts to feel physically uncomfortable, like pressure building up inside me. I’ll end up standing up, walking to the printer, or finding some reason to move, even if I don’t really need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as acting impulsively a lot, at least not in a way that stands out to me. I’d probably need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I catch myself jumping in before someone’s finished, especially with junior auditors. If I think I know the answer or I have an idea, it feels urgent in my head, and I’ll blurt it out before I really stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in other situations?\n\nPatient: Yes. Waiting is hard for me. Like if I’m in line at the courthouse records office or sitting in a clinic lobby, I get really tense and impatient. I keep checking my phone, or I’ll go over what I need to say in my head instead of just waiting calmly.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though what I remember more is what teachers wrote about me. Even in elementary school, my report cards would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through small details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still jokes that I could ace a test but forget the project sitting in my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I can get frustrated or overwhelmed, but I don’t usually feel like my mood is suddenly shifting in a big way for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much. It goes back to elementary school, from what I remember and from what my report cards said. Teachers would say I was bright, but I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to finish my work. My mom still teases me that I could ace a test and then forget the project sitting right in my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your schoolwork when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember that being a problem, and honestly it still is. I can know exactly what I’m supposed to do and still miss a small detail—like a changed date, transposing a number, or leaving some placeholder note in a workpaper. It’s embarrassing, especially in accounting, because details are supposed to be my strength.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused you significant problems or stress in your personal or work life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s caused real problems. At work, the mistakes and the things I don’t follow up on have gotten serious enough that a client review was affected. And now I’m worried people are questioning whether I can really handle the promotion.\n\nAt home, it causes stress too. My husband has ended up taking over bills and commitments I agreed to handle, because he can’t really count on me to finish them reliably. It’s frustrating and embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties affected your relationships with friends or family in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with my husband. He’s had to take over bills and things I had agreed to handle because I don’t always follow through reliably. It makes him frustrated, and I feel embarrassed because it probably seems like he can’t count on me.\n\nWith work, too, it affects how people see me. Some mistakes and unfinished follow-up got bad enough to affect a client review, and now I worry people are questioning whether I can handle the promotion.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these same difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity in both your work and personal life consistently over the years?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been showing up in both places, but the point where it really started interfering was about a year and ten months ago. That was after I got promoted following a firm-wide award, and suddenly the workload and responsibilities were a lot harder to juggle. Since then it’s been much more consistent and noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently since childhood, without any significant periods where these issues were not present?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even back in elementary school, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I lost assignments, rushed through details, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to actually finish my work.\n\nMy mom still jokes that I could do well on a test but forget the project sitting right in my backpack. So it’s not new, really. It’s just caused bigger problems as an adult.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Some mistakes and unfinished follow-up got bad enough to affect a client review, and now I worry people are questioning whether I can handle the promotion.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these same difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity in both your work and personal life consistently over the years?\n\nPatient: Yes.
